‘BYo 


Press Information 


The BYU Football Media Guide is designed to provide 
the media with useful information about the 1991 BYU foot- 
ball team. The guide contains statistics, records, biographi- 
cal data and historical facts. The following information also 
will help the media in covering the Cougars’ during the 
1991season. Please review it and take note of changes. 


Player Interviews 

Please schedule all player and coach interviews through 
the Sports Information Office. To assure a better chance of 
obtaining the interview, please notify the Sports Informa- 
tion Office at least 24 hours in advance of the interview. 
Coach LaVell Edwards is available to media from 10-11 
a.m. Members of the print media requesting interviews 
should contact Ralph Zobell, Sports Information Director. 
Requests for broadcast interviews should be made to Jay 
Monsen, Electronic Media Coordinator. No player inter- 
views will be given the day before, or on, game day. 


Big Four Huddle 
Coach Edwards and two football players attend the Big 
Four Huddle at noon each Tuesday at the Red Lion Inn in 
Salt Lake City. The coach and players will be available for 
individual interviews immediately after the luncheon. 


Home Game Coverage 

Requests for press box and photography credentials 
should be made no later than one week prior to the 
game. These credentials will be issued to working mem- 
bers of the media only, with preference given to media rep- 
resentatives who regularly cover college football. No 
requests will be processed later than noon on the day prior 
to the game. Requests should be written on company let- 
terhead and directed to: BYU Sports Information, 30 SFH, 
Brigham Young University, Provo, UT 84602. Phone (801) 
378-4911. 


Stadium Location, Media Parking 
BYU's stadium is located on the northwest corner of the 
campus, just off Canyon Road and University Parkway 
(1430 North) in Provo. Press parking is in Lot #2, immedi- 
ately west of the stadium near the ticket booth. Media park- 
ing passes must be requested. The press box is accessible 
via elevators located on the west side of Cougar Stadium. 


Press Box Arrangements 

The Sports Information staff will provide working media 
members with official statistics. A press box public address 
announcer will keep the media informed of play-by-play in- 
formation, broken records and other interesting informa- 
tion. A meal will be served before each home game. 

Several telephones (campus extensions) will be avail- 
able in the main area of the press box. Collect or credit 
card calls can be made from these telephones. In the past, 
there have been sufficient phones to meet the needs of the 


media. However, if guaranteed availability is desired, 
visitors may wish to order their own phones. Please notify 
the Sports Information Office in advance if FAX transmis- 
sions are necessary. The press box staff will be glad to as- 
sist in transmitting copy and statistics. Score updates are 
available by calling (801) 378-2609. 


Photography Credentials 

Photo credentials will be issued to newspapers and 
magazines which regularly cover college football. Mark 
Philbrick, BYU's photographer, will supervise the distribu- 
tion of photo vests at the game. Photographers on the 
sidelines must wear the required vest at all times. 
Television photographers and still cameramen are not al- 
lowed on the sidelines between the 25-yard lines. Please 
use north elevator to access press box. Pick up photo 
vests at the press box and return them after the game. 


Post-Game Interviews 

In order to provide equal access to BYU athletes for 
post-game media interviews, BYU’s locker room will be 
closed to all members of the media for both home and 
away games. Post-game interviews at home games will be 
conducted in the new Press Interview Room, located east 
of BYU's locker room (under the east stands). Interview re- 
quests should be made to members of the BYU Sports In- 
formation staff, who will accompany the media to the Press 
Room. With two minutes remaining in the game, the media 
group will take the south elevator to ground level. 


The Press Interview Room will be equipped with an 
audio mixer for those desiring to record the interviews. 
Also, there will be sufficient lighting available for television 
cameras. Coach LaVell Edwards will speak to the media 
after the mandatory 10-minute cooling off period, which 
begins after the team enters the locker room. Media repre- 
sentatives can request that players be brought into the 
Press Room for interviews after Coach Edwards has 
finished speaking. Quotes from both coaches will be avail- 
able as soon after the game as possible for those unable 
to get to the interview room. 


Fan Hotline 
BYU fans can get up-to-date Cougar sports information 
each day by calling 378-TEAM. The hotline is sponsored 
by Universal Campus Credit Union and features scores 
and statistics from previous athletic events, information 
about upcoming events, and general information about the 
men’s and women’s athletic programs. 


More Information 
For pictures, media guides, video tapes, and other infor- 
mation, contact: BYU Sports Information, 30 SFH, BYU, 
Provo, UT 84602. Phone: (801) 378-4911.

1991 Outlook 


Rebuilt lines and the toughest schedule in school 
history raise questions about Brigham Young 
University’s 1991 football prospects despite the return 
of the top quarterback in college history. 

For the first time since 1978, a Heisman Trophy win- 
ner returns for a chance to repeat. Ty Detmer, who 
threw for the most yards ever with 5,188 while 
winning the Heisman Trophy and a stadium full of 
other awards and 
trophies, is recovering 
from shoulder surgery 
after two separations in 
the Holiday Bow! loss. 

“Having Ty back is a 
great place to start," says 
Coach LaVell Edwards. 
Detmer should be ready 
to start throwing in May 
and needs just 446 
yards passing to set 
the NCAA career yard- 
age record. The senior 
passing wizard from San 
Antonio, Tex., has set 42 
NCAA records and tied 
five others. 

A key loss is consen- 
sus All-America tight end 
Chris Smith, who had 
1,156 yards in receptions- 
-the highest total ever by 
anyone at his position. 

Add to the graduates 
four-year starter Matt Bel- 
lini at halfback, four of the 
five offensive linemen 
and all three down 
linemen on defense. 

Coach Edwards 
begins his 20th season 
in the Disneyland Pigskin Classic against Florida 
State, a team expected to be ranked as high as first 
in a number of pre-season polls. 

The Cougars then travel to UCLA and Penn State 
before getting into Western Athletic Conference rivals 
like bowl winners Colorado State and Air Force along 
with Wyoming, a narrow loser in bowl competition. 

A bright spot is finally getting to play Hawaii in 
Provo. Other reasons for optimism include the return 
of offensive guard Bryan May and fullback Peter 
Tuipulotu. 


Abig upset, like the one over Miami last season, 
won't happen at home since the Cougars open with 
Florida State, UCLA and Penn State on the road. 


On defense, honors’ candidates include linebacker 
Rocky Biegel and defensive backs Tony 
Crutchfield and Derwin Gray. Other starters return- 
ing are linebackers Shad Hansen, Scott Giles and 
Jared Leavitt (the latter two shared the 1990 season 
at one outside backer spot). 

Punter/kicker Earl Kauffman returns for his senior 
year. He would have led the nation much of last year 
but didn’t qualify because 
of too few kicks. 

The Cougars do have a 
lot of size among the offen- 
sive linemen but not much 
experience. And the receiv- 
ing corps--traditional un- 
sung heroes in the Air 
Edwards formula--has 
only Bryce Doman who 
has started a game at 
BYU and he’s been away 
from football two years on 
a church mission. 

A large group of fresh- 
men redshirts plus an ex- 
. cellent crop of frosh 
recruits, especially running 
backs, could help BYU's 
quest for its third straight 
’ WAC title. BYU has won 

the conference title 12 of 
the last 15 years. 

Coach Edwards con- 
tinues as the third win- 
ningest active coach 
(175-59-1) behind 
Nebraska's Tom Osborne 
and Joe Paterno of Penn 
State. 

Here’s a closer look at 
the Cougars: 

OFFENSE 
Backfield 

Detmer has the pins out of his throwing shoulder 
and is undergoing therapy. Without him, Cougar 
coaches got a look at backup senior Joe Evans, 
sophomore Brock Spencer (last year’s JV signal 
caller), transfer Garth Fennegan (Clemson) and red- 
shirt freshman Ryan Hancock, the league’s Fresh- 
man of the Year as a pitcher on the baseball team. 
Back from church missions are Tom Young and 
Scott Shreeve. In the fall, high school phenom John 


mI 


last fall and then sprained an ankle and missed half of 
spring practice. At the other tackle spot letterman 
Tom Ladd will try and hold off star of the future Eli 
Herring (6-6, 310), a returned missionary. Line coach 
Roger French says, "There aren't many athletes in 
the country better than Herring and Empey." 
Receivers 

Trying to replace Smith won't be easy at tight end. 
The candidates are Matt Zundel with good hands 
and Byron Rex with good speed. BYU might go with 
three wide receivers more often than in recent years. 
Micah Matsuzaki and Nati Valdez should have good 
years and Eric Drage has worked hard to make the 
conversion from halfback to wideout. Erik Hughes 
from the track team provides speed and two of the 
best frosh receivers ever--Doman and Tyler Ander- 
son--will be back from missions but missed spring 
practice. 


Kicking Game 

Earl Kauffman, a three-year kickoff man and two- 
year punter, returns and should be an honors’ can- 
Walsh will be here. Hancock was impressive in didate. Deep snapper Bruce Jenne returns. 
bouncing back and forth between football spring drills 
and baseball practice. 

Tuipulotu was BYU's top rusher last year (637 
yards). Although he averaged only 53.1 yards per 
game he scored nine touchdowns and had an impres- 
sive per-carry mark of 6.5. Scott Charlton has been 
moved from fullback to halfback to try Bellini’s spot, 
which was primarily a pass-catching role last season. 

Eric Mortensen seems at full strength after a 
series of injuries and could spell either starter. Look- 
ing good in the spring were Jason Schmidt, Steve 
Christensen and Hema Heimuli, highly recruited 
before going on a mission out of high school. 

Some standout incoming freshmen also could 
make the traveling squad from a group Edwards calls, 
"as good a group of athletes as we've recruited." 

Offensive Line 

Only Bryan May returns but there are 12-15 
players vying for starting spots and the competition in 
the spring was intense. 

May probably will move to guard and the other 
guard could be redshirt freshman Evan Pilgrim, 
returned missionary Mike Empey (6-6, 280) or senior 
Garett Tujague. 

The center job is a battle between Mike Jenkins (6- 
7, 340) and squad member Garry Pay. Returned mis- 
sionary Ron Simmons is another possibility at a 
position which is a concern for the Cougars after the 
loss of three-year starter Robert Stephens. 

Redshirt transfer Scott Brumfield (6-8, 320) is bat- I 
tling letterman Kent Griffith for one tackle spot. 

Brumfield was a JC All-American but broke his wrist 


Safety Josh Arnold returns for his senior season. 


| ee 


Micah Matsusaki should score touchdowns in 1991. 


Vying for Andy Boyce's spot as the holder are Joe 
Evans and Josh Arnold. 

DEFENSE 
Defensive Line 

"Interesting," is the way Coach Edwards refers to 
his biggest problem area--at least on paper. The top 
four players are gone and part-timer Brad Hunter 
had ankle surgery in the spring. Chris Conners played 
well in limited action last year but newcomers could 
determine how strong the Cougars are on defense. 

Redshirts Randy Brock (6-7, 260) and Scott Sral- 
la (6-4, 265) are battling for the tackle spots as are 
freshmen returned missionaries Greg Pitts and 
Doug Stone. 

Another redshirt, sophomore Lenny Gomes, could 
start at nose guard backed up by freshman Harland 
Ah You. Conners has switched to nose guard and 
could be a factor. 

Linebackers 

The strength of the team going into 1991 is the 
linebacking corps, where only Alema Fitisemanu is 
gone. Jared Leavitt is back at one outside spot after 
neck surgery and his replacement, Scott Giles, could 
be on the other side. 

Rocky Biegel will be starting for the fourth year 
and Shad Hansen is back at the other inside spot. 
Kevin Nicoll, a transfer who redshirted last year, had 
a good spring as did squad member Mike Hoggan 
and Todd Herget. Redshirt freshmen Justin Yarro 
and Travis Hall also could see action. 

Secondary 

Three players who started at the end of the season 
are back although cornerback Tony Crutchfield is 
coming off knee surgery. Josh Arnold looks set at 


strong safety and 
Derwin Gray at 
weak safety. In 
addition, Ervin 
Lee played a lot 
last year in the 
nickel defense 
and had a key in- 
terception 
against Miami. 

But some new- 
comers could 
make big 
splashes. 
Corners Paul 
Pitts and 
Courtney Over- 
street, both from 
Pasadena City 
College, have ex- 
cellent speed and incoming frosh Hassan-Kareem 
McCullough brings standout credentials. Add to the 
list freshman Karlos Rhodes and there's good depth 
in the defensive backfield. Rhodes was on campus 
for spring practice and could make a big contribution 
if he regains top speed after knee surgery. 

Special Teams 

Stacey Corley, who had most of the kickoff returns 
is gone but the next four--Mortensen, Charlton, Gray 
and Brad Clark--are back. The two punt returners-- 
Bellini and Mike Salido --are gone so the Cougars are 
looking at Clark, Matsuzaki, Kapi Sikahema and 
Patrick Mitchell, a redshirt freshman who also could 
see action as a defensive back. 


Brad Clark was voted top special 
teams’ player for last season. 


rate 


Scott Charlton moves to halfback in a role which saw Matt Bellini become BYU’s all-time leading receiver.

The "Other" Tie 

The only tie in LaVell Edwards’ career 
came in his 26th game as head coach on 
Oct. 5, 1974, at Fort Collins. A 26-6 lead 
had dwindled to 33-27 with six seconds 
left and BYU had the ball. But the final 
snap was bobbled and Colorado State got 
a chance for one more play--which went - 
for a touchdown. The ensuing celebration 
caused a 15-yard penalty and resulted in 
the PAT barely missing. However, con- 
fusion resulted when Rams’ players and 
fans misinterpreted the official's end-of- 
game signal as indicating the kick was 
good. Ironically, BYU went from 0-3-1 to 
win seven straight and make its first post- 
season appearance--in the Fiesta Bowl.

The Heisman Hero 


" For the 56th time since Jay Berwanger 
won the award in 1935, it is the special 
privilege of the Downtown Athletic Club 
and its membership composed of thousands 
of men and women, residents throughout 
these United States, to join me in letting the 
country know the name of this year’s 
greatest football player--the winner of the 
1990 Heisman Award...whose name is Ty 
Detmer...Ty Detmer of BYU, Ty Detmer!" 
C. Peter Lambos 
Downtown Athletic Club 
New York City 
Dec. 1, 1990

TYLER ANDERSON* 
Wide Receiver 

6-2, 170, So. 

Orem, Utah 

Personal: Born 4/27/70 in Salt Lake 
City...parents are Roland and Mary 
June...father ran track for BYU in the late 
1950s...older brother Brian played bas- a A 
ketball for Utah Valley Community Col- 
lege...had a 4.0 GPA as a senior...served a church mission 
to Louisiana 1989-90...recruited by Tennessee in track and 
in football, Arizona State Washington, UCLA, Stanford and 
Notre Dame...majoring in business. 

High School: WAS MVP at Orem High in football and 
track and was the school’s athlete of the year...was a wide 
receiver and free safety...helped lead team to 4A champion- 
ship and was all-state...in track he broke the state record 
with a 10.96 100 meters as a junior...as a junior he hada 
wind-aided 21.64 in the 200 meters which unofficially broke 
the state record...placed second in the 100 and 200 in 
state as a senior...football coach was Tom Rabb (Southern 
Utah). 

BYU: 1988: Had a 40-yard reception at Hawaii...had a 
nine-yard TD catch from Ty Detmer against New 
Mexico...caught a 41-yard TD pass to give BYU a halftime 
lead at the Air Force Academy...had a 52-yard reception at 
Utah. 1991: Returned from mission just as spring drills 
were ending.

Personal: Born 8/31/70 in Bethesda,

Utah State...married Melinda Peay in ie 
April, 1991...majoring in sports medicine. 

High School: Led Dobson High to 1987 state champion- 
ship...lettered four times in soccer and three times in 
track...was an all-league goalie and an all-state running 
back...second in state 110-meter hurdles and fourth in pole 
vaulting...coached by Mike Clark. 

BYU: 1988: Played in junior varsity games and traveled 
with the varsity...had two tackles, caused a fumble and in- 
tercepted a pass. 1989: Was a second unit player on the 
varsity...intercepted a pass against CSU. 1990: Had a 19- 
yard interception at UTEP...10 tackles and a pass deflec- 
tion against Miami...nine tackles against San Diego 
State...six tackles, a pass interception and a sack at 
Oregon...had a 27-yard interception at Utah plus seven un- 
assisted tackles and a fumble recovery...returned an inter- 
ception 31 yards for a TD against Utah State...selected as

ROCKY BIEGEL*** 

Linebacker 

6-1, 225, Sr. 

Wisconsin Rapids, Wisconsin 
Personal: Born 12/13/68 in New Lon- 

don, Wisc..,...real name is 

Rockne...parents are Kenneth and 

Jeneane...married Jamie Dempze in 

January, 1990...younger brother T.D. is a 

running back for BYU, but is now ona 

church mission...recruited by UCLA, Nebraska, Michigan, 

Michigan State, Wisconsin and Oklahoma...majoring in 

communications with a minor in business. 


High School: Earned 10 letters at Lincoln High...state 
champion wrestler as a junior and senior...holds state 
record for pins (97) and most wins...pinned every opponent 
as a senior...holds school record in high hurdles...state 
player of the year in football...first-team Bally All- 
American...Dial National High School Athlete of the Year 
finalist...Gatorade Circle of Champions Player of the Year 
in Wisconsin...played quarterback as a junior...coached by 
his father. 

BYU: 1988: Saw a lot of playing time due to in- 
juries...had 21 tackles and a pass interception. 1989: 
Started 10 games but injured his knee in the fifth game of 
the season against Wyoming...had his knee scoped and 
returned by the Oregon game...had 15 tackles against Air 
Force. 1990: Second team All-WAC...had eight tackles, 
two quarterback hurries and a sack against Miami...11 tack- 
les and two quarterback hurries at Oregon...had an inter- 
ception, eight tackles and a quarterback hurry against 
Colorado State...against Utah he had eight assisted tack- 
les, caused and recovered a fumble and had a quarterback 
hurry...had eight tackles and three quarterback hurries 
against Utah State...selected as coaches player of the 
game against San Diego State, Oregon and Utah. 1991: 
Had off-season shoulder surgery.

SCOTT CHARLTON** 
Halfback 

6-0, 215, Sr. 

Idaho Falls, Idaho 

Personal: Born 5/8/69 in Oakland, 
Calif...parents are Tom and Darlene ...a 
highly recruited for baseball...recruited by 
Utah and Washington State...majoring in 
civil engineering. 

High School: Lettered twice in foot- 
ball, three times in baseball and once in track...all-state in 
football his senior year...hit .417 in baseball and placed in 
the 100 and 200-meter dashes in the state track meet as a 
junior...rushed for 1,285 yards on 181 carries and scored

he was coached by Ralph Hunter.

ing a freak collarbone break...was a reserve on the varsity.

two-yard TD pass at Oregon...caught a six-yard TD against 
Colorado State...rushed for 53 yards and scored the final 
touchdown at Air Force...had a 39-yard catch at Utah...had 
a 10-yard TD catch against Utah State...had a 59-yard kick- 
off return at Hawaii as one of his four returns in that game.

JOHN CHRISTENSEN* 
Defensive Back 

5-11, 185, Sr. 

Buena Park, California 

Personal: Born 5/31/69 in Long 
Beach, Calif.,...parents are Clyde and Lor- 
retta Grimwood...was recruited by 
Washington State, Oregon, Las Vegas, 
Sonoma State, and Long Beach State, 
Fullerton State...majoring in sociology. 

High School: Prepped at John F. Ken- 
nedy High...all-county in football and baseball, made both 
all-star teams...was a centerfielder and running back and 
safety...MVP of the Orange County All-Star game in foot- 
ball...set a record for an 80-yard run from scrim- 
mage...class president of sophomore, junior and senior 
classes and also most preferred man each of those 
years...coached John Carroll (Montana).

against Colorado State...selected as coaches’ special

the Holiday Bowl. 1991: Had off-season knee surgery.

WAC Adds Fresno State 
Fresno State will become the 10th member of the 
Western Athletic Conference on July 1, 1992. Original 
members were BYU, New Mexico, Utah and Wyoming. 
Colorado State and Texas-El Paso joined in 1967, San 
Diego State in 1978, Hawaii in 1979 and Air Force in 1980.

TY DETMER*** 
Quarterback 
6-0, 175, Sr. 
San Antonio, Texas 

Personal: Born 10/30/67 in San Mar- 
cos, Texas...parents are Sonny and 
Betty...father is now coaching at Mission, 
Texas...recruited by Miami, UCLA, 


Michigan, Texas A&M...committed to BYU in fall of senior 
year...married Kim Herbert in July, 1991...younger brother 
Koy is a major college prospect as a high school quarter- 
back...majoring in recreation administration. 

High School: Texas Player of the Year and prep All- 
America at Southwest High, where he was coached by his 
father...lettered three times in football, basketball, baseball 
and golf, plus once in track...all-state in baseball and all- 
district in basketball...ranks high in all-time high school 
passing stats. 

BYU: 1987: Redshirted. 1988: Most Valuable Player in 
Freedom Bowl after taking BYU from a 14-7 deficit to a 20- 
17 win...started against New Mexico and led Cougars to 
65-0 win...against Lobos hit 24 of 35 passes for 333 yards 
and five touchdowns...relieved in second half several times 
as he shared duties with Sean Covey...was 10 of 20 for 
238 yards against Utah...connected on 16 of 27 for 212 
yards at Miami...for the season was 83 of 153 with 13 
touchdowns and 10 interceptions, four of those were in the 
opener at Wyoming. 1989: Led the Blue to 28-12 win in the 
spring game with 23 of 32 for 289 yards and two touch- 
downs...perhaps the first sophomore to ever be elected as 
a co-captain...finished number one in the NCAA for pass ef- 
ficiency at 175.6 and second to Andre Ware in total offense 
at 364.42...his 576 yards passing and 594 yards total of- 
fense in last year’s Holiday Bowl against Penn State estab- 
lished new records for all-time bowl performances in the 
NCAA...was co-MVP of the Holiday Bowl...was first team 
All-WAC by the coaches and writers...WAC offensive 
player of the week against UTEP, CNN player of the week 
against San Diego State and Chevrolet player of the game 
against the Air Force Academy...broke 11 NCAA 
records...selected as team MVP. 1990: Broke 31 more 
NCAA records, including 5,188 passing yards, for a total of 
42 NCAA records broken and five tied...Heisman Trophy 
winner, but was in Hawaii when the award was announced 
via satellite hookup...awarded Maxwell Trophy and Davey 
O'Brien Award...player of the year by CBS, UPI, Football 
News, Scripps Howard and the Western Athletic Con- 
ference...first team All-America by Associated Press, UPI, 
Football Writers, Walter Camp, Football News, Scripps 
Howard and The Sporting News...unanimous first-team All- 
WAC...when he engineered the upset over number one 
Miami with a career high 38 completions, he was named 
player of the week by the WAC, CNN, Football News and 
Athlon...against San Diego State he was given the CBS 
Toyota Leadership Award and named Chevrolet Player of 
the Game and WAC player of the week...was named WAC 
player of the week against Air Force...the U.S. Sports 
Academy named him Amateur Athlete of September and 
amateur athlete of the year, as did the Deseret 
News...twice he threw five TD's in a game against 
Washington State and New Mexico...selected as team co- 
captain and voted MVP...had his throwing hand in a remov- 
able cast before and after the Oregon game...suffered a 
slight shoulder injury against Utah...suffered two separated 
shoulders in the Holiday Bow! against Texas A&M...the left 
shoulder was separated in the second quarter when he 


19 


was taken to the locker room with 5:50, but did not miss a 
series...the right shoulder was separated with 12 minutes 
left in the third period and he did not return to the game. 
1991: Had surgery on his right shoulder in January...did 
not play in spring practice...needs 426 yards to become 
the NCAA career passing leader.

Second Kickoff Classic 

This year’s Disneyland Pigskin Classic against Florida 
State will mark the second time BYU has played in a kick- 
off event. Coming off the national championship, BYU 
played in the Kickoff Classic in 1985. 

Playing in front of a national television audience, the 
Cougars defeated Cotton Bowl champion Boston College, 
28-14, at Giants’ Stadium in East Rutherford, New Jersey. 

Robbie Bosco was named MVP by completing 35 of 53 
passes for 508 yards. Glen Kozlowski had a personal best 
10 catches for 241 yards. The Eagles’ Mike Roth had 12 
unassisted tackles and four sacks to lead the Eages. 

With the score deadlocked at 14 in the third quarter, 
safety Mark Sherman made a diving interception, which 


28 


led to the Cougars’ go-ahead touchdown. 


years...team MVP and most inspirational player...Chamber 
of Commerce Best Athlete Award...coached by Austin Mc- 
Naughton (BYU). 

BYU: 1987: Saw most of his playing time with the junior 
varsity. 1990: Redshirted...had shoulder surgery. 1991: Im- 
pressive in spring scrimmages.

Graduate Assistants 

Robert Anae is in his second year as a graduate as- 
sistant and helps with the offensive line. He is working 
on a Ph.D. in sociology. 

An offensive guard on BYU's 1984 national champion- 
ship team, Anae was second team All-WAC. He at- 
tended Kahuku High in Laie, Hawaii, where he was 
all-state in football and all-region in baseball. 

After serving an LDS mission to Tulsa, Okla., he en- 
rolled at BYU and later received a bachelors’ degree in 
political science (1986). 

He completed a master’s degree at the University of 
Hawaii (1988), where he assisted with the football pro- 
gram. He and Leane have two children: Famika and 
Penny. 

Andy Boyce, a first team Academic All-America, 
moves into coaching after gaining more yardage (1,241) 
in 1990 than any receiver in BYU history. 

Astar in football, basketball and track at Olympus 
High in Salt Lake City, Boyce served an LDS misssion in 
the West Indies. 

After redshirting a year he developed into one of the 
Cougars’ all-time great receivers and held on place kicks 
the past two years. 

He and Lesli are expecting a child in December. He 
is enrolled in the MBA program after majoring in com- 
munications. 

Tom Holmoe enters his second season as a 
graduate assistant after seven years and three Super 
Bowl championships ('84, 88, ’89) with San Francisco in 
the National Football League. 

He kept busy in 1990 helping with the secondary and 
travelling with the varsity after Dick Felt’s heart surgery. 

The native of La Crescenta, Calif., was a defensive 
back at BYU from 1978-82, earning All-WAC honors his 
senior year. He was picked in the fourth round by the 
49ers. 

Holmoe graduated in 1983 in zoology and pre-med 
and is currently working on a master's degree in athletic 
administration. 

He and Lori have three children: Shannon, Danny 
and Erik. 

Ed Larson also is in his second year as a G.A. and 
will work with quarterbacks and receivers. He will com- 
plete a master’s degree in athletic administration in 
December. 

While a quarterback at New Mexico, he received a 
bachelors’ degree in university studies (1988). It was 
Larson who started the 1988 game against BYU in 
Provo in place of injured Jeremy Leach. Ty Detmer made 
his first start that day in place of an injured Sean Covey. 

Larson grew up in San Jose, Calif., where he at- 
tended Piedmont Hills High and San Jose City College. 
He led the Jaguars to the 1986 California JC title. 

He and Jeanne, have three children: Tiffany, Cassey 
and Chase 

(Editor's Note: At press time, the junior varsity 
schedule was still being finalized. There also was the 
possibility of the Cougars adding a fifth G.A.)

Mantle of a Champion 


By Michael L. Benedict 


His name is LaVell. 

On the sidelines he stands—arms folded, temples thinning, hair 
graying—gazing outward, in pursuit of victory. 

His are many. From the early days of his career as a high school 
coach, through “The Edwards Era" at Brigham Young University, his 
contests have proven themselves to be reflections of the man, his 
character. 

His has been portrayed in the dignity with which he has savored 
victory, for he realizes that in another locker room, is another coach 
and team, whose efforts have fallen short of stopping his frightening 
offense and relentless defense. 

He knows the fruits of victory are bittersweet, for often have been 
the times that he, too, has returned to a silent locker room, disap- 
pointed though graceful in the face of defeat. He has understood as 
well as any that a win and a loss are to be accepted with equal 
courage, knowing that only one is desirable, though both are in- 
evitable. 

His has been portrayed as he has sought to instill in each player 
integrity for self, respect for opponent and regard for spectator. Such 
has taken him from the ranks of obscurity to the pinnacle of promi- 
nence as he and his Cougars have placed themselves among the 
elite in college football. From his devotion to principle, those who see 
him on the sidelines know that he looks beyond the scoreboard and 
the record book to measure success. 

The passing of each season of football marks for him a passing of 
aseason of life—full of adversity and triumph. As he exemplifies that 
one must conquer both to be a champion, all regard him as a man of 


stature as well as statistics; a family man as well as a coach; acitizen 
as well as a competitor. His reflection shows all that the victories on 
the field are of the moment, and the greatest victory of all is to master 
life itself. 

Silently he stands, the intensity of the moment reflected in his 
gaze. The mantle of his character, the mark of a champion.

SEPNDDAONS 


‘Bie 


LaVell Edwards, Head Coach 


When LaVell Edwards was named BYU’s head football 
coach in 1972, Cougar football fortunes skyrocketed. 

Prior to '72, BYU had averaged less than three wins a year 
in the school’s 47 football seasons. The Cougars could claim 
only one Western Athletic Conference championship (1965) 
and had never been to a bowl game. 

Since that time, however, BYU has won 175 games, 13 
WAC championships (including 10 in a row from 1976-85) 
and played in 15 bowl games (including 13 in a row). Honors 
and recognition have rolled in as the football program has 
risen to national prominence. 

Six times in his 19 years as head coach ('72, ‘79, '80, '83, 
’84 and '90), the 60-year-old Edwards has been selected 
WAC Coach-of-the-Year, and five times he has been named 
Kodak District Eight Coach-of-the-Year ('74, '77, '79, '80 and 
'84). The post-season games that LaVell has coached in 
include the Hula Bowl (twice), the Blue-Gray Classic (twice), 
the East-West Shrine game (three times), the Japan Bowl 
(twice) and the Coaches’ All-America Game. 

In 1988, LaVell completed a term as president of the 5,700- 
member American Football Coaches Association (AFCA). He 
was the 64th president in the association's history. 

In 1984, Edwards led the Cougars to their first-ever football 
national championship and along the way, picked up NCAA 
Coach-of-the-Year honors from the American Football 
Coaches, the Football Writers and the Pigskin Club of 
Washington, D.C. 

Such accolades are justly deserved for one of the great 
minds in the coaching profession who also is a churchman, 
husband, father, grandfather, author, teacher and doctor. 

LaVell’s winning percentage (.747) ranks third among the 
nation’s active college coaches. And under Edwards, BYU 
has averaged over 10 wins a season in the 1980's. In 1986, 
LaVell passed Frank Kush as the all-time leader in WAC 
coaching victories. 

Having roots in Orem, Utah, LaVell was born and raised not 
far from BYU's stadium. He graduated from Lincoln High 
School and attended Utah State, where he was a three-year 
letterman and captain of the football team. Twice he earned 
all-conference honors at center for the Aggies. 

At USU he met his wife-to-be, Patti Covey. LaVell entered 
the Army, where he played on two service football teams: Fort 
Lee, Va., in 1952 and Fort Mead, Md., in 1953 as a player- 
coach with a 6-3 record, 

From there, LaVell went into his first full-time coaching job 
at Granite High School in Salt Lake City, where he coached 
from 1954-61 before joining the BYU staff in 1962. Ten years 
after becoming part of the Cougar staff, Edwards was named 
head coach, succeeding Tommy Hudspeth. 

The football philosophy practiced by Coach Edwards is one 
of high-powered offense and rugged defense. Under his guid- 
ance, BYU has led the nation in passing eight times ('76, '77, 
‘79, '80, '81, '83, 84 and '85), in total offense five times ('79, 
'80, '83, '84 and '85) and in scoring ('79). 

A 1952 graduate of Utah State, Edwards has added to his 
academic honors a master's degree from the University of 


175-59-1 
20th Year 
(Utah State ’52) 


Best time to call Coach Edwards: 10 a.m. 


37 


Utah (1960) and an Ed.D. from BYU (1978). His attitude 
toward education is contagious, and several of his assistants 
have gone on to attain doctorate degrees. 

Edwards has coached more years, won more games and 
had a better winning percentage than any coach in Cougar 
football history. His wife, Pat, writes columns for the Provo 
Daily Herald. They have three married children: Ann Edwards 
Cannon, a writer, Salt Lake City; John, a doctor, New York 
City; and Jim, a lawyer, Las Vegas. 


Personal Data 


Born Oct. 11, 1930, in Provo, Utah . . . attended schools in 
nearby Orem . . . was all-state at Lincoln High School (now 
Orem High) in 1946 and 1947. .. three-year regular at center 
. .. also lettered two years each in basketball and track . . . 
enrolled at Utah State in 1948, played center and linebacker 
. . . three-year letterman as center for the Aggies . . . was 
captain and all-conference for two years (1950-51)... 
played service ball for the Army (1952-54) at Fort Lee, VA... 
was player-coach for 6-3 team at Fort Meade, MD... returned 
to Utah and joined staff at Granite High in Salt Lake City 
(1954), serving as head football coach, wrestling coach and 
golf coach for a period of eight years . . . also tennis and 
basketball for two seasons (1954-55) . . . joined the BYU staff 
in 1962... selected as the Bobby Dodd National Coach of the 
Year and Churchmen’s Hall of Fame Coach in 1979... 
selected NCAA Coach-of-the-Year in 1984 by Football 
Coaches, Football Writers and the Washington, D.C. Pigskin 
Club . . . led BYU to National Championship in 1984... 19 
seasons as BYU's head coach .. . teams have completed an 
overall record of 175-59-1 .. . (7-4 in 1972; 5-6 in 1973; 7-4-1 
in 1974; 6-5 in 1975; 9-2 in 1976; 9-2 in 1977; 9-4 in 1978; 
11-1 in 1979; 12-1 in 1980; 11-2 in 1981; 8-4 in 1982; 11-1 in 
1983; 13-0 in 1984; 11-3 in 1985; 8-5 in 1986; 9-4 in 1987; 9-4 
in 1988; 10-3 in 1989 and 10-3 in 1990) .. . in 1974 he 
received the coveted Dale Rex Memorial Trophy and was 
honored as Utah Sportsman of the Year... obtained an M.S. 
degree from the University of Utah in 1960 and an Ed.D. from 
BYU in 1978. .. married the former Patti Louise Covey of Big 
Piney, Wyoming, and they have three children; Ann Cannon, 
awriter; John, a doctor and former member of the BYU track 
team; and Jim, a lawyer and former BYU football player . . . 
LaVell is one of the best golfers among the nation’s football 
coaches . .. has nine grandchildren.

LaVell’s Career Highlights 


1972 LaVell Edwards, defensive coordinator for the Cou- 
gars is named head coach in January .. .had been a member 
of the BYU staff since 1962 . . . halfback Pete VanValken- 
burg wins NCAA rushing title with a 138.6 yards-per-game 
average . . . the Cougars also lead the league in defense 
against the rush. 

1973 The Cougars begin to move offensively . . . quarter- 
back Gary Sheide is runnerup in NCAA passing and No. 3 in 
total offense . . . teammate Jay Miller leads the nation in pass 
receiving (100 receptions for 1,181 yards) . . . Cougars finish 
second nationally in passing and eighth in total offense. . . 
Miller sets an NCAA record for most pass receptions (22) in 
one game (record still stands). 

1974 Team ranks high in two NCAA statistical categories; 
seventh in passing offense, fourth in rushing defense... Gary 
Sheide is runnerup in passing for the second year in a row 
(behind Steve Bartkowski of California) . . . Gary’s career 
completion percentage is fourth on the all-time list. . . 
Bartkowski’s final pass completion edged Sheide (182-181) 
for title. LaVell wins first WAC championship and a trip to the 
Fiesta Bowl against Oklahoma State. 

1975 A year of adjustment, the Cougars falter through the 
first part of the season, then win six of their last eight games 
. . . Gifford Nielsen, a sophomore reserve, comes off the 
bench in the fourth game (New Mexico) to spark a come-from- 
behind victory . . . Giff sets WAC record with 10-of-12 passing 
in that game . . . becomes starter . . . Cougars finish 10th in 
team passing. 

1976 Gifford Nielsen places fourth in NCAA passing 
(18.8) with only one-tenth completion separating him from 
Jack Thompson (Washington State) in second place . . . Giff 
finishes second to Tommy Kramer (Rice) in total offense . . . 
Cougars finish first in passing, sixth in total offense and eighth 
in NCAA scoring (424.4 total, 307.8 passing and 31.9 scoring) 
... Nielsen voted first team All-American . . . Cougars tie for 
WAC championship and play Oklahoma State in the Tanger- 
ine Bowl. 

1977 Gifford Nielsen unexpectedly exits scene with injury 
in fourth game (Oregon State) and Marc Wilson takes over... 
by the end of the year, the two have combined for 40 TD 
passes, 3,585 yards passing and a .605 completion percent- 
age . . . Wilson finishes eighth in passing and third in total 
offense . . . Cougars lead nation in passing (341.6), finish 
second in scoring (39.4) and third in total offense (470.2)... 
BYU's 341.6 yards per game is third highest in history, and 41 
TDs second . .. Cougars top the nation in “takeaways” with a 
total of 56 defensive takes (fumbles recovered or intercep- 
tions) .. . Cougars tie for first in WAC and make post-season 
trip to Japan. 

1978 Both Marc Wilson and sophomore quarterback Jim 
McMahon see action in 10 games as they share quarterback 
duties . . . Wilson is injured in the third game of the year... 
neither quarterback is mentioned in NCAA stats, but Cougars 
are eighth in passing and fourth in fumble recoveries . . . 
Cougars win WAC and play in first Holiday Bow! against 
Navy. 

1979 With two all-conference quarterbacks to choose 
from, Edwards decides to redshirt McMahon in favor of se- 
nior Wilson . .. Cougars upset Texas A&M in season opener 


and go on to record 11 straight wins . . . Wilson leads the 
nation in total offense (325.5) and is fourth in pass efficiency 
(147.1)... finishes the year as consensus All-American with 
10 NCAA records to his credit . .. Cougars lead the nation in 
four categories: passing (368.3), total offense (521.4), scor- 
ing (40.6) and kickoff returns (26.3) . .. also, the Cougars are 
11th in scoring defense (11.4) and sixth in punting (40.1) . . . 
BYU becomes first division | school to pass for more than 
4,000 yards in a season. . . LaVell receives the Bobby Dodd 
coach-of-the-year award . . . Cougars win WAC again and 
play Indiana in Holiday Bowl! Il. 

1980 The school's first 12-win season . . . also, BYU leads 
the nation in three statistical categories; total offense (535.0), 
scoring (46.7) and passing (409.8) . . . Quarterback Jim 
McMahon is named to the Kodak All-America team along 
with tackle Nick Eyre ... McMahon is the first Division | player 
to pass for more than 4,000 yards in a season (4,571)... he 
setor tied 26 NCAA records, mostin total offense and passing 
... his pass efficiency (176.9) was the highest ever... he also 
led the nation in total offense (385.6) . . . after losing the first 
game to New Mexico, BYU wins 12 straight... Cougars win 
WAC again and play SMU in Holiday Bowl III. 

1981 Another 11-win season for the Cougars .. . have a 
17-game win streak and rank eighth in the polls before losing 
to Nevada-Las Vegas . . . BYU leads the nation in passing for 
the third consecutive year... McMahon earns All-America 
honors and finishes his career with 71 NCAA records (plus 
the first Davey O'Brien Award) and sets record with all-time 
high in career pass efficiency (156.9) . . . LaVell coaches 
West team to a victory in the Hula Bowl... also coaches in the 
Japan Bowl... Cougars win WAC again and play Washington 
State in Holiday Bowl IV . . . Edwards’ 86-32-1 record ranks 
among top ten in the nation. 

1982 BYU expands Cougar Stadium to 65,000 seats and 
sells out each game .. . lose home opener to Air Force (39-38) 
but come back to win seventh straight WAC title and play Ohio 
State in Holiday Bowl V . . . Gordon Hudson is named 
consensus All-America . . . BYU finishes third nationally in 
total offense . . . Steve Young is named WAC offensive 
player-of-the-year. 

1983 Another 11-winseason...Cougars are ranked No. 7 
in final polls, highest ever for a BYU team . . . quarterback 
Steve Young and tight end Gordon Hudson both named 
consensus All-Americans . . . after losing to Baylor in the 
opening game, BYU wins final 11 games ... once again, BYU 
leads the nation in passing and total offense and is second in 
scoring... Cougars set NCAA total offense record with 584.2 
yards-per-game . . . BYU wins eighth straight WAC title and 
defeats Missouri in Holiday Bowl VI. . . Edwards surpasses 
the 100-victory milestone. 

1984 Cougars win the National Championship by becom- 
ing only the third team in NCAA history to win 13 games in a 
season . . . win streak is extended to 24 games. . . quarter- 
back Robbie Bosco leads the nation in passing and finishes 
second to Doug Flutie in pass efficiency (151.8) . . . defen- 
sive back Kyle Morrell named first team All-America... team 
leads the nation in passing (346.2) and total offense (486.5) 
. .. defensively, Cougars finish 10th in scoring defense . . . 
another WAC championship and victory over Michigan in 


Holiday Bowl VII... Edwards named NCAA coach-of-the- 
year by most organizations. 

1985 Cougars’ defense of the National Championship be- 
gins with win over Boston College in the Kickoff Classic . . . 
defensive tackle Jason Buck is named third team All-Amer- 
ica and WAC defensive player-of-the-year . . . Mark Bellini 
earns second-team All-America honors . . . a tie with Air Force 
for the WAC championship . . . quarterback Robbie Bosco 
finishes the season ranked second in total offense and sev- 
enth in pass efficiency . . . he finishes third in the Heisman 
Trophy balloting . .. BYU defense ranks 19th against the rush 
and 18th overall . .. Cougars lose to Ohio State in the Florida 
Citrus Bowl and end the season ranked 16th in both polls. 

1986 Edwards passes Frank Kush as the winningest 
coach in WAC football history . .. Jason Buck becomes only 
the third player in the Western United States to win the Out- 
land Trophy and is named consensus All-American . . . 
Shawn Knight is also named to several All-American teams 
. .. Lakei Heimuli comes on strong toward the end of the 
season to break Jeff Blanc’s record for career rushing yards 
. .. Cougars fail to win WAC championship for the first time in 
11 years but still post an 8-5 record and play UCLA in the 
Freedom Bowl. 

1987 BYU leads the nation with 50 turnovers (28 intercep- 
tions and 22 fumble recoveries) with Troy Long getting the 
most defensive points . . . Cougars rebound to rank ninth 
nationally in passing offense and 22nd in scoring offense. . . 
after offensive line is decimated in pre-season camp, Cou- 
gars bounce back from 1-2 start to win eight of final nine 
regular season games . . . young backfield shows promise of 
things to come in getting within a two-point conversion of 
another WAC title . . . Chuck Cutler begins the first of two 
years as a GTE Academic All-America first teamer ... Leon- 
ard Chitty sets career records for consecutive conversions 
(42) and for field goals (34) . .. Cougars win Gridiron Bow! in 


Melbourne, Australia, but injuries prove costly in All American 
Bowl loss to Virginia. 

1988 Cougars win seven straight but drop to third in WAC 
race . . . Punter Pat Thompson named All-America and 
finishes second nationally while team is first in punting and 
net punting . . . behind quarterbacks Sean Covey and Ty 
Detmer, BYU is fourth in passing, 10th in total offense and 
13th in scoring . . . Cougars play second-ranked Miami on 
even terms for three quarters . .. Bob Davis is a finalist for the 
Butkis Award as the country's top linebacker . .. 11th straight 
bowl trip is 20-17 upset over Colorado. 

1989 BYU returns to the top in the WAC winning the WAC 
title for the first time since 1986 . . . Offensive lineman 
Mohammed Elewonibi becomes the second BYU player to 
win the Outland Trophy in the past four years and is named 
consensus All-America . .. Bob Davis and Chris Smith are 
also named to several All-America teams . . . Freddie Whit- 
tingham is named to the first team Academic All-America 
team . . . Sophomore quarterback Ty Detmer breaks 11 
NCAA records and ties two others . . . Cougars make 12th 
straight bowl appearance, but drop a tough game to Penn 
State in the Holiday Bowl. 

1990 Ty Detmer becomes the first player in the Inter- 
mountain area to win the Heisman Trophy. He finishes the 
year with 5,188 yards passing—the most ever and increases 
his NCAA records to 47. . . Chris Smith is a concensus 
All-America and has more reception yards (1,156) than any 
tight end in history . .. Andy Boyce gains more yards receiv- 
ing (1,241) than anyone who has played at BYU. . . Matt 
Bellini breaks the career record for catches with 204 . .. The 
Cougars shock the nation by upsetting top-ranked Miami 
but lose a shot at a number-one ranking with a loss at Oregon 
... but BYU wins the WAC and goes to its 13th straight bowl 
game.

Assistant Coaches 


Claude Bassett (BYU ’78) 


Inside Linebackers 

Claude Bassett enters his 
ninth year with the Cougar foot- 
ball program. He was named an 
assistant coach in 1985 after 
working as a graduate assistant 
with the Cougars for two years. 

Prior to coming to BYU, Bas- 
sett was a coach and teacher at 
Mountain View High School in 
Mesa, Ariz. There he worked 
under Coach Jess Parker and 
helped the team win the state 
title twice. He also taught and 
coached at Pleasant Grove High (1976) and Spanish 
Fork High (1978) in Utah County. 

Born in Reno, Nev., Bassett attended Enterprise 
High School in Redding, Calif., where he participated 
in football and baseball. He also attended Shasta 
Junior College in Redding. 

Before transferring to BYU (where he graduated in 
1978), he served a two-year mission for the LDS 
Church in Rome, Italy. He is married to the former 
Leslie Johnson of Orem, and they have four children: 
Jed, Annette, Camille and Rebecca. 


Robbie Bosco (BYU ’86) 


Offensive Assistant 

Robbie Bosco begins his 
second year as a full-time assis- 
tant after being a graduate assis- 
tant in 1989. The Roseville, 
Calif., native is best known for 
leading BYU to the national 
championship in 1984, when he 
guided the Cougars to a 13-0 
™ record and led the nation in 

passing. 

~ — In 1985, he finished second in 
4 NCAA total offense and was 

third in the Heisman Trophy bal- 
loting for the second straight year. He set 10 NCAA 
record at BYU, was a second team All-America, and 
finished third in voting for the Davey O’Brien Trophy. 

Drafted by the Green Bay Packers, Bosco had his 
pro career cut short by an arm injury. He graduated 
with a degree in communications and is now working 
on a master's degree in physical education. He is 


a 


married to Karen Holt and they have two children, 
Amber and Karissa. 


Norm Chow (Utah ’68) 


Quarterbacks, Receivers 

Norm Chow has been an in- 
tegral part of BYU's successful 
offense for the past 14 seasons. 
He coaches the receivers and 
the quarterbacks and is the 
coach who calls BYU’s plays. 
For several years, Chow coor- 
dinated BYU's recruiting efforts. 

Prior to joining the BYU 
coaching staff fulltime, he spent 
two years as a graduate assis- 
tant. Chow graduated from 
Punahou High ('64) in Honolulu, 
where he was named "Athlete of the Year." 

He then attended the University of Utah, where he 
achieved All-WAC (1967-68) and honorable mention 
All-America honors as an offensive guard. He 
graduated in 1968. 

A career in the Canadian Football League was cut 
short after one year when Chow injured his knee. He 
then became head coach for three years at Hawaii's 
Waialua High School. 

Chow received a master’s degree in special educa- 
tion from the University of Utah and a doctorate in 
educational psychology from BYU in 1979. He and 
his wife, Diane, have four children: Carter, Maile, 
Cameron and Chandler. 


Dick Felt (BYU ’58) 
Asst. Head Coach, Secondary 


After an outstanding college career at BYU anda 
seven-year stint in professional 
football, Dick Felt returned in 

1967 to become a coach. 

This is his 24th year on the 
staff. After returning from heart 
surgery at the start of last 
season, Felt’s responsibilities 
continue to be with the defen- 
sive backs. 

Dick lettered in five sports at 
Lehi High School in Utah and 
was an all-state football player. 
He went to BYU where, as a 


sophomore, he came off the bench in a game with 
San Jose State to score a record four touchdowns in 
one quarter. He won all-conference honors as a 
senior. 

After graduating in 1958, he played for Bolling Air 
Force Base (1958-60), where he earned all-service 
honors and was named MVP in the Air Force (1960). 
He was a first lieutenant. 

Felt played two seasons with the New York Titans 
(1960-61), the second year attaining all-pro second- 
team honors as a defensive back. He was traded to 
the Boston Patriots and was all-pro in 1962. He 
played in the first two AFL all-star games. Felt left pro 
ball in 1966 and came to BYU in 1967. 

Dick is married to Dayleen LaVon Hobson of Boise, 
Idaho, and they have four children: Karen, Curtis, Kris- 
tie and Nancy. 


Roger French (Minnesota ’58) 
Offensive Coord., Off. Line 


Roger French has been in charge of BYU's offense 
for the past 11 years, during 
which time the Cougars have 
traditionally led the nation in 
passing and total offense. 

French joined Edwards’ staff 
in 1980 and has since helped 
produce numerous All-WAC and 
All-America offensive linemen. 
including 1989 Outland Trophy 
winner Moe Elewonibi.. 

Before coming to BYU, Roger 
coached at Minnesota and Wis- 
consin for 14 years. His respon- 
sibilities at Minnesota included 
coaching the offensive line, tight ends and receivers. 
He was also defensive coordinator and line coach at 
Wisconsin and Memphis State. 

Since French has come to BYU, the Cougars have 
won the National Championship (1984) and set the 
NCAA total offense record of 584 yards per game 
(1983). They also led the nation in passing and total 
offense three of the last seven years. 

Born in Minneapolis, French played linebacker, 
tight end and fullback at Minnesota, graduating in 
1956. He obtained a master’s degree and played with 
the Philadelphia Eagles and the Cleveland Browns. 

He began coaching at Memphis State, where he 
was defensive coordinator. In 1962, MSU finished 
second in the nation defensively. He has coached 
several All-American linemen in his career, and more 
than 35 have gone on to play pro ball. 

Roger and his wife, Dody, have two daughters, 
Kathy and Gail. 


Chris Pella (Utah State ’66) 


Recruiting Coord., Kicking, TE’s 
Chris Pella joined the BYU 
coaching fraternity in 1986 after 
spending 18 years as a coach 

at Utah State. He was head 
coach of the Aggies from 1983- 
85. 

A native of Brigham City, 
Utah, Pella attended Box Elder 
High School and Dixie Junior 

College, where he was team 
ma captain and a junior college All- 
= a American. He then joined the 
Utah State Aggies as a fullback, 
offensive lineman and kicker, setting a school record 
with nine PATs against New Mexico State. 

After graduating in 1965, Pella was hired as an as- 
sistant coach, a position he held for 15 years under 
coaches Tony Knap, Chuck Mills, Phil Krueger and 
Bruce Snyder. 

Over that time, he worked with every position on 
the team. He also was head wrestling coach for two 
years. Pella worked for the U.S. Navy in Yokosuka, 
Japan, as the athletic director and football coach at 
the Navy base from 1969 to 1972. His 1971 team 
won the Interservice Championship and played in the 
Rice Bowl. 

He returned to Utah State in the fall of 1972. In 
1983 he was named head football coach, replacing 
Bruce Snyder. 

Pella and his wife, Rita, are the parents of three 
children: Brian, Jake and Nicole. 


Tom Ramage (Utah State ’57) 


Defensive Line, Special Teams 

Tom Ramage has perhaps 
the most varied football coach- 
ing background in the state of 
Utah. He joined the BYU staff 
18 years ago after coaching in 
the high school, junior college 
and major college ranks. 

Since Ramage was hired, the 
Cougars have traditionally 
ranked high in the WAC in 
defense against the rush. 

Ramage came to BYU from 
Dixie College in St. George, 
Utah, where he was head coach for two seasons (13- 
7 overall). He was born in Price, Utah, and enrolled at 
Utah State, where he won All-Skyline Conference and 
All-America honors as a tackle in 1956 and lettered in 


42 


wrestling four years. He graduated in 1957. 

He began his coaching career at Bear River, Utah, 
then returned to USU, where he served as assistant 
and obtained his M.S. degree (1962). For the next 
four years, Tom was line coach of the Aggies under 
Tony Knap. 

He left Logan in 1965 to serve as chief assistant 
and defensive coordinator at Weber State under Sark 
Arslanian. In 1971 he took the head job at Dixie Col- 
lege. Sandwiched into his stay at Weber, he served 
as a commissioned officer in the Army and was dis- 
charged as a captain in 1967. Tom has coached two 
Outland Trophy winners: Merlin Olsen (Utah State) 
and Jason Buck (BYU). 

Tom and his wife, Winona, have six children: John, 
an assistant coach at Utah State; Lorrie; Rochelle; 
Sherrie; Dedra; and Cory. 


Lance Reynolds (BYU ’77) 


Running Backs 

For more than a decade, 
Lance Reynolds has played a 
key role in BYU's football suc- 
cesses as aplayer and as a 
coach. He joined the BYU staff 
in 1983 after a successful 
coaching job at Snow College 
(Ephraim, Utah). 

A Salt Lake native, Lance was 
all-state at Granite High School. 
He played offensive tackle and 
guard at BYU before leaving on 
an LDS mission to Seattle, 
Wash.., after his sophomore year. 

When he returned, he earned All-WAC honors at of- 
fensive tackle and played in the East-West Shrine 


game and the Senior Bowl. After graduating in 1970, 
he played one season with both the Pittsburgh 
Steelers and Philadelphia Eagles. 

Lance and his wife, Leslie, have five children: Brit- 
tany, Lance Jr., Dallas, Matthew and Houston. 


Ken Schmidt (Utah ’64) 


Def. Coord., Outside Linebackers 

Ken Schmidt takes over the 
title of defensive coordinator 
after serving in that role last 
season while Dick Felt under- 
went heart surgery. 

Ken joined the Cougars in 
1982 after a successful high 
school and junior college coach- 
ing career. 

A Murray, Utah, native, 
Schmidt enrolled at the Univer- 
sity of Utah, where he was a 
center and linebacker under 
Coach Ray Nagel. He graduated in 1964 and ob- 
tained his M.S.degree from Utah in 1969. 

He began his coaching career as an assistant at 
Granger High School (1964-68). Later, he was named 
head coach at Skyline High School (Salt Lake City), 
where his teams posted a 105-24-1 record and won 
five state championships during his stay (1968-79). 

In 1980, he was named head coach at Ricks Col- 
lege (Rexburg, Idaho). The Vikings won the con- 
ference championship both his years there and 
posted a 17-3 record. In 1981, Ricks defeated 
Arizona Western in the Valley of the Sun Bowl. 

Ken and his wife, the former Yvonne Youngberg of 
Murray, have five children: Ryan, Craig, Jason, 
Shauna and Chad.

‘Bio 


Brigham Young University 


Brigham Young University is a private, church-sponsored 
institution which has earned an admirable reputation for 
successfully blending scholarly learning with moral and spiri- 
tual education. 

BYU began modestly in 1875 as Brigham Young Academy 
with 29 elementary school students. It is now a fully accred- 
ited, internationally recognized university with a student 
body of 27,000, making it the largest private university in the 
nation in terms of full-time students. 

Academic excellence is increasingly evident as students 
and faculty show their competence. Almost three-quarters of 
the 1,300 full-time faculty hold doctoral degrees, and the 
university is accredited by more than 100 education, busi- 
ness, professional and government organizations. 

Admission standards for students continue to rise as com- 
petition for acceptance increases. The average ACT com- 
posite score for entering BYU freshmen is now 23, compared 
to the national average of 19. BYU graduated four Rhodes 
Scholars between 1975 and 1978, a record matched only by 
Harvard, Yale and Princeton. In 1976, it was one of two 
schools to place three Danforth fellows in a single year. 


The J. Reuben Clark Law School is establishing a reputa- 
tion as one of the elite top-40 law schools in the nation, with 
three of its graduates already selected as clerks to the U.S. 
Supreme Court. 

Other highly respected programs include the School of 
Management, with its fifth-ranked accountancy department 
and top-rated MBA program; the computer-aided design/ 
computer-aided manufacturing program, with state-of-the- 
art computer ability and a reputation for innovation; a large 
team of researchers in several disciplines studying coal's 
promise as an alternative to oil; the Ezra Taft Benson Agricul- 
ture Food Institute, which is contributing knowledge to help 
solve the world’s food problems. 

The music and drama departments are also particularly 
noteworthy, as are its language programs, with their unusu- 
ally extensive course offerings, and a large number of faculty 
researching family issues. 

To fill the 14 semester hours of religion required, students 
can take courses ranging from the history of the LDS Church 
to comparative world religions. Four of the 14 credit hours 
are spent in study of the Book of Mormon, a text regarded by 
Mormons as scripture, along with the Bible. 

The 631-acre campus is a superb facility. It is unusually 
clean and well-kept, with beautiful landscaping and spacious 
quads. The Lee Library, with more than two million volumes 
and a seating capacity of 4,000, is at the heart of the campus. 

The Harris Fine Arts Center features five theatres, two art 
galleries, nine pipe organs, 65 music practice rooms, a Na- 
tional Public Radio station and a Public Broadcasting Ser- 
vice television studio. Laboratories all over campus boast 
up-to-date equipment and full computerization. A new art 
museum is now under construction. 

In addition to the Utah and Hawaii campuses, BYU main- 
tains Study Abroad programs in China, Austria, England, 
France, Italy, Israel, Canada, Mexico, Brazil and Jerusalem. 

Academic accomplishments and physical facilities aside, 
many BYU students and faculty point to the character of its 
people and their commitment to traditional values as the 
school’s most important attributes. Each student signs a 
Code of Honor, promising to abstain from alcohol, tobacco, 
tea and coffee and to be honest and chaste—all in accord 
with tenets of the LDS Church. 

Although 98 percent of the student body is Mormon, ad- 
mission is open to those of any race, color, creed or national 
origin, provided they meet the university's academic require- 
ments and their conduct conforms to the standards of the 
LDS Church. 

Non-Mormons come from over 25 other denominations, 
and about 22 percent of the student body is married. Stu- 
dents come from every state (32 percent from Utah) and 
more than 70 countries. More than 8,000 students are bilin- 
gual, having learned a foreign language while serving a 
mission for the LDS Church in another country. 

BYU is on a three-semester system (4-4-2-2) with fall and 
winter semesters each being four months long, and the 
summer semester being divided into spring and summer 
terms. 


44 


‘Bie 


President Rex E. Lee 


Perhaps the best example of President Rex E. Lee's na- 
tional reputation came earlier this year to chair a committee to 
review the NCAA's rules enforcement and infractions pro- 
cess. Among those serving under him is Warren E. Burger, 
former Chief Justice of the United States. 

Lee, 56, a former U.S. Solicitor General, became the 10th 
president of Brigham Young University on July 1, 1989. He 
was the founding dean of the J. Reuben Clark Law School at 
BYU, serving from 1972 to 1981. From 1985-1989 he was 
the law school’s George Sutherland Professor of Law. 

He was U.S. Solicitor General from 1981 to 1985. Pre- 
viously, from 1975-1976, he had served as Assistant U.S. 
Attorney General in charge of the Justice Department's Civil 
Division. 

Lee is a 1960 graduate of Brigham Young University and 
earned a juris doctorate from the University of Chicago in 
1963. He served as law clerk to Justice Byron R. White of the 
U.S. Supreme Court, 1963-1964, and then practiced law with 
the firm of Jennings, Strouss and Salmon in Phoenix from 
1964-1972. 

It was during his tenure as founding dean of the law school 
at BYU that Lee took a leave of absence to serve as Assistant 
U.S. Attorney General. 

Since 1985, he has been a partner in the law firm of Sidley 
and Austin, working out of its Washington, D.C.., office. 

Earlier in his career, he served as a lecturer for the Ameri- 
can Institute of Foreign Trade, 1966-1968, and from 1968 to 
1972 he was on the faculty of the University of Arizona School 
of Law. 

He has been admitted to the bar in Utah, Arizona and 
Washington, D.C., and is a member of the American Law 


Institute. In late April, 
1990, he argued his 
50th case before the 
U.S. Supreme Court. 
He is one of only a 
handful of living law- 
yers to have reached 
that milestone. 

The university presi- 
dent has served his 
church as amissionary 
in Mexico, where he 
was second counselor 
to the mission presi- 
dent; as a member of 
the Young Men Gen- 
eral Board (1958— 
1960), and has been 
a bishop, stake presi- 
dent, stake high coun- 
selor, Sunday School 
teacher, and in other administrative and teaching positions. 

As a young boy growing up in St. Johns, Arizona, he earned 
the distinction of being the first boy in that community to 
become an Eagle Scout. 

Rex Edwin Lee was born in Los Angeles, Calif., Feb. 27, 
1935, a son of Rex E. and Mabel Whiting Lee. His father died 
before Lee was born, and his mother married Wilford J. 
Shumway two years later. Lee married Janet Griffin in the 
Mesa, Arizona, LDS Temple in 1959. They have seven chil- 
dren and five grandchildren. 


R. J. Snow, Student Life Vice President 


R. J. Snow is currently serving in his 
second year as Student Life Vice 
President at BYU. He accepted the 
position after returning from the South 
Africa Johannesburg Mission where 
he served as mission president from 
1987-1990. 

Before his mission call, Snow was 
president and director of The Jacob- 
sen Company from 1984—1987. Prior 
to that, he served for nearly a decade 
as the vice president of university and student relations at the 
University of Utah. 

He also served as director of the Robert H. Hinckley Insti- 
tute of Politics and as a member of the political science 
faculty during his tenure at Utah. 

A native of St. George, Snow received his associate of 
science degree from Dixie College in 1957. He then enrolled 
at the University of Utah where he earned both his bachelor 
(1962) and masters of arts (1964) degrees in history. 

Snow continued his education at Northwestern University 


45 


where he completed his doctoral degree (1965) in political 
science. 

In 1967, Snow moved to California where he served on the 
political science faculty for the University of California, Santa 
Barbara, until 1973. 

Snow has also completed post-doctoral work at the Uni- 
versity of Oregon, Harvard University and has worked with 
the University of Pau and the University of Bordeaux as part 
of a UC Santa Barbara study abroad program. 

Snow has been very active in civic and community service 
having served on the committee for Executive Reorganiza- 
tion of the State of Utah, as board member of the Utah Sym- 
phony and on task forces for the homeless and mentally ill. 

His service on more than 25 other civic and charitable 
organizations include board membership of the Salt Lake 
United Way, and board chairman for the Utah affiliate of the 
American Diabetes Association. 

Born in Cedar City as Reuben Joseph Snow on October 
15, 1937, he and his family moved to St. George when he 
was six months old. In 1962, he married the former Marilyn 
Melville in the St. George Temple. They have four children.

Athletic Personnel 


Pete Witbeck, Associate AD 

Associate Athletic Director Alan "Pete" Witbeck 
been involved with BYU athletics either as an athlete, 
as a coach or as an administrator for 42 years. 

Witbeck, a native of Raymond, Alberta, Canada, 
came to BYU in 1949 as a long distance runner on 
the track team. While in Provo, he acquired 
bachelor’s (1952) and master’s (1954) degrees 
before joining the PE. staff in 1955. 

Three years later, Witbeck was hired to coach the 
freshman basketball team, a position he held for six 
years. During that time, his teams posted a 62-9 
record, losing only three times in the last three years 
Witbeck was coach. 

The 1963-64 Kittens were undefeated and ranked 
first in the nation among freshmen teams. That group 
went on to win the NIT title in 1966. 

Witbeck was given the prestigious Dale Rex 
Memorial Award, awarded annually to the person who 
has contributed most to amateur athletics in the State

g 


Athletic Department 


In keeping with the tradition of excellence established by 
the University’s academic programs, BYU's Athletic De- 
partment has become known around the country for its 
successes in various sports. 

BYU has finished in the top-20 in overall national rank- 
ings for 12 of the last 20 years. The Cougars have finished 
in the top-10 nine times and in the top-5 five times. In the 
Western Athletic Conference, the Cougars have won the 
all-sports supremacy title 14 straight years. 

The University's athletic program boasts 12 NCAA- 
sanctioned sports: football, basketball, baseball, indoor 
and outdoor track, golf, cross country, tennis, gymnastics, 
swimming, wrestling and volleyball. 

Besides football, the golf (1981) and track (1970) teams 
have won national championships. Cougar basketball 
teams have won the NIT twice (1951, 1966) and have been 
ranked in the top-20 several times. They reached the final 
eight in the NCAA playoffs in 1981. BYU's baseball team 
won 19 WAC division titles in 20 years and was ranked as 
high as No. 1 nationally in 1983. 

Part of the success of these teams can be attributed to 
the fine athletic facilities at BYU. The football team plays its 
games in the beautiful 65,000-seat Cougar Stadium. Prac- 
tices are held on the field north of the George Albert Smith 


Cougar fans will enjoy this 10th season in the expanded Cougar Stadium this fall. 


50 


it 


Fieldhouse. The practice area incorporates four full-size 
football fields and other workout stations. The team’s 
locker room and training facilities are located inside the 
Smith Fieldhouse. 

The fieldhouse contains facilities for nearly every sport. 
A one-fifth mile rubberized track surrounds three basket- 
ball courts, weight rooms and an artificial turf practice area. 
The west annex of the fieldhouse is used by baseball, 
football, track and golf teams for indoor practice. 

Attendance at BYU basketball games consistently ranks 
among the best in the nation. Games are played in the 
spacious 23,000-seat Marriott Center, the second largest 
on-campus basketball arena in the country. The facility is 
also used for concerts, forums and other activities. 

BYU's track is acclaimed by coaches and athletes as 
one of the finest in the nation. The NCAA track and field 
championships were held there in 1982 and 1989. All run- 
ning and field events can be viewed from the grandstand 
area. An animated electronic scoreboard entertains fans 
and keeps athletes current with running times, splits, etc. 

The BYU baseball diamond is adjacent to the Marriott 
Center, the football stadium and the track and field com- 
plex. Bleachers provide seating for as many as 4,000 
people. 


‘Bey 4 Strength and Conditioning Center 


BYU's strength and conditioning center is one of the best in the country. 


BYU's conditioning facility ranks among the best in the 
country. 

With nearly 10,000 square feet, the state-of-the-art center 
features 106 stations enhanced by beautiful blue and gray 
carpeting and by a stereophonic sound system with 40 speak- 
ers. 

In short, it's an addition which looks good, sounds good and 
provides plenty of room for intercollegiate athletes to work 
out. 

Patterned after the best features of weight rooms at other 
major football powers, the BYU facility is both functional and 
attractive. Its biggest advantages over previous conditioning 
areas for BYU athletes are that variety and ample space are 
provided. 

Athletes no longer have to wait around for a certain piece of 
equipment. They can get more done in much less time than 
before and do not have to plan their weight training in early 
morning or late evening to get it all in. 


51 


That is especially helpful for athletes of other sports who 
have had a struggle finding equipment available during foot- 
ball season. 

Another plus for the athletes is the motivational factor of 
getting caught up in conditioning fever by seeing so many 
others working out at the same time. Cougar athletes who 
tried the new facility shortly after it opened during the summer 
raved about how much more fun it was to work on strength 
and mobility in large, rather than small, groups. 

Athletes also have been quick to point out the recruiting 
benefits of the new facility. They insist the BYU conditioning 
setup is far better than any they have encountered during 
visits to other universities. 

Even coaches of sports where athletes traditionally don’t 
look first at weight rooms feel the new facility will be of benefit. 
For one thing, it shows BYU is serious about conditioning and, 
once an athlete is here, he or she will improve by being more 
involved in weight training.

EN 


When BYU expanded its football stadium to 65,000 seats in 
1982, it marked the beginning of a new era for the Cougar 
football program. 

In each of its first nine seasons the stadium has been sold 
out with season tickets. Last year 396,011 fans watched the 
Cougars play at home, an average of 66,002 per game. BYU 
has ranked near the top 20 in the country in football atten- 
dance every year since the expansion. Arizona State is the 
only Western school which draws larger crowds than the 
Cougars. 

The fan support has helped BYU schedule home-and- 
home non-league games with teams like Miami, UCLA, 
Texas, Penn State, Alabama, and Notre Dame. 

Since its expansion, the facility has been applauded by 
fans, players and the media as one of the best in the country. 
Among its many features are a box-bowl configuration, a 
natural turf playing surface and a four-level press box. 

Prior to 1982, the stadium seated 35,000 comfortably, but 
as many as 47,000 crammed into the facility to see a game. 
Expansion efforts included lowering the field eight feet, 
adding end zone seating, constructing the press box and 
adding box seating in the West stands. 

More than 15,000 yards of concrete were used in the ex- 
pansion effort. Each end zone contains about 4.3 million 
pounds of steel and seats about 15,000 people. 

The playing field has a special sand-based drainage sys- 
tem capable of disposing two inches of rainfall per hour. 

Perhaps the most impressive feature of the stadium is the 
press box, which runs the entire length of the west stands and 
rises over 10 stories high. It contains 42 loges (glass enclosed 
booths capable of seating up to 12 people) on the first two 
levels. The third level is reserved for working media, and the 
fourth level is the TV deck. Three elevators transport guests 
to the press box. 

In 1982 and 1988, the Football Writers Association of Amer- 
ica cited BYU for its “Outstanding Press Box Working Area.” 


53

for an unobstructed view. Adjacent to the Smith Fieldhouse is the 
indoor tennis complex (right). BYU’s baseball field (below) seats 4,000.

largest pre-season crowd in 
NBA history.

Cougar Club 


In just over 25 years, BYU’s Cougar Club has 
grown into one of the nation’s most prestigious and ef- 
fective booster organizations. 

Its membership of more than 
4,000 also makes the Cougar Club 
one of the nation’s largest booster 
clubs and nearly twice as large as 
any other booster organization in 
the Western Athletic Conference. 

"Most of our members are avid 
sports fans and die-hard Cougar 
supporters," explains Dale R. Mc- 
Cann, Cougar Club executive direc- 
tor. "However, all our members 
agree that affecting the lives of 
young male and female athletes in a positive way is 
our first priority.” 

Some of the Cougar Club services include funding 
recruiting trips, sponsoring the spring football game 
and the "Varsity Preview" basketball game and honor- 
ing present and past athletes at the annual Awards 
Banquet and through the BYU Athletic Hall of Fame. 

Cougar Club members donate annually to the BYU 
athletic program. There are numerous donor levels, 


Dale McCann 


“6 


ranging from $50 for Cougar "Cubs," children and 
teenagers, to $1000 for Golden Cougars. Lifetime 
memberships are also available. 

In return, contributors receive parking passes for 
home games, football and basketball press guides, 
monthly news releases, weekly press releases (out-of- 
state members only), the satisfaction of helping to 
build athletic excellence at BYU, and other benefits. 

Interested persons are invited to visit the Cougar 
Club room (west side of Cougar Stadium), where they 
can view the Athletic Hall of Fame, and meet Cougar 
Club officials. They may also write of call: BYU 
Cougar Club, Alumni House, Brigham Young Univer- 
sity, Provo, Utah 84602, (801) 378-BLUE. 

Much of the tremendous growth is the result of the 
efforts of McCann, who has been executvie director 
since 1975. 

A former cheerleader at West High in Salt Lake 
City, he graduated from BYU in business manage- 
ment (1966). 

Prior to working at BYU he was employed at 
General Dynamics and Signetics. He is married to the 
former Andrea Schmidt. They have 10 children and 
four grandchildren.
